Achieving Better Local Regret Bound for Online Non-Convex Bilevel Optimization
Tingkai Jia, Haiguang Wang, Cheng Chen

Abstract
Online bilevel optimization (OBO) has emerged as a powerful framework for many machine learning problems. Prior works have developed several algorithms that minimize the standard bilevel local regret or the window-averaged bilevel local regret of the OBO problem, but the optimality of existing regret bounds remains unclear. In this work, we establish optimal regret bounds for both settings. For standard bilevel local regret, we propose an algorithm that achieves the optimal regret Ω ⁡ ( 1 + V T ) \Omega(1+V_{T}) with at most O ⁡ ( T ​ log ⁡ T ) O(T\log T) total inner-level gradient evaluations. We further develop a fully single-loop algorithm whose regret bound includes an additional gradient-variation terms. For the window-averaged bilevel local regret, we design an algorithm that captures sublinear environmental variation through a window-based analysis and achieves the optimal regret Ω ⁡ ( T / W 2 ) \Omega(T/W^{2}) . Experiments validate our theoretical findings and demonstrate the practical effectiveness of the proposed methods.



1
Introduction

Online bilevel optimization (OBO) has become a powerful framework for a wide range of machine learning problems.
In this work, we study non-convex-strongly-convex online bilevel optimization (OBO), defined as follows:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\min_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}F_{t}(\mathbf{x}):=f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right)\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),\end{split}

(1)

where the upper-level function
f_{t}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth but possibly non-convex, the inner-level function
g_{t}:\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
is smooth and
\mu_{g}
-strongly convex which ensures the existence of a unique minimizer
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
for all given
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
. The existing OBO algorithms
35
;
25
;
4
;
30
are built apon hypergradient-based approach, which has been proven to be efficient and powerful in offline bilevel optimization cases
9
;
21
;
6
;
16
.
It requires a nested iterative process in each step
t
to solve for the inner solution
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
, then the hypergradient
\nabla F_{t}(\mathbf{x})
can be calculated using the approximate implicit differentiation (AID)
or iterative differentiation (ITD)
technology.

), (

), Theorem

and (

), respectively. We prove the standard bilevel local regret upper bounds of OBBO and SOBOW with window size
w=1
in Appendix
B.6
and Theorem
B.16
. More details about the upper bound of SOGD with no noise are given in Appendix
B.7
.

3.3

Existing OBO studies mainly focus on two regret notions.
(
35
;
25
;
4
)
consider a window-averaged notion of local regret to cope with time-varying objectives in the online setting. This strategy was initially proposed by
(
15
;
1
;
13
)
in online single-level optimization. However, their theoretical results still do not get rid of sublinear assumption of some environmental variation regularities
7
;
2
;
18
;
37
.
(
30
)
first analyze the OBO problem under standard bilevel local regret without window averaging. But their results rely on the stronger assumption of many sublinear environmental variation regularities and do not appear to be optimal.

1.1
Our Contributions

This work analyzes both the standard and window-averaged bilevel local regret, proposes novel algorithms, and discusses optimal bounds under both settings.

Standard bilevel local regret minimization.

We propose a novel adaptive inner-loop online bilevel optimizer (AOBO) shown in Algorithm
1
. It tracks the drift of the subproblem optimum
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
by dynamically adjusting the number of inner iterations. Our analysis on the standard local regret shows that AOBO achieves the optimal bound under the mildest possible assumptions on environmental variation. A simple fully single-loop online bilevel optimizer (FSOBO) is also proposed, presented in Algorithm
2
, achieving the minimum computational cost and a bound including additional gradient-variation terms.

Window-averaged bilevel local regret minimization.

We introduce a new window-averaged bilevel local regret to evaluate algorithmic performance under worst-case linearly changing environments. Our regret analysis is the first to completely remove the reliance on the sublinear environmental variation assumptions. Building on this regret, we propose an Window-averaged Online Bilevel Optimizer (WOBO) shown in Algorithm
3
. It captures the sublinear variation within a time window of length
w
, and attains an upper bound of
O(T/W^{2})
. Moreover, we establish a matching lower bound of
\Omega(T/W^{2})
, which implies that our result is optimal.

1.2
Related Work

Bilevel Optimization.
The bilevel optimization (BO) problem was originally formulated by
(
5
)
. Existing gradient-based algorithms can be broadly categorized into two classes: (1) the AID based methods
31
;
11
;
10
;
12
;
21
;
32
; (2) the ITD based approach
29
;
8
;
33
;
28
;
21
. Several works reformulate the bilevel problem into a constrained single-level optimization by replacing the inner solution with its optimality conditions
39
;
22
;
27
;
26
;
34
. In parallel, Hessian- or Jacobian-free methods circumvent the explicit computation of second-order derivatives by using zeroth-order approximations—such as finite differences—to estimate the hypergradient
36
;
20
;
38
.

Online Bilevel Optimization.
Compared to BO, OBO remains relatively underexplored.
(
35
)
and
(
25
)
were the first to investigate OBO and introduced the window-averaged hypergradient framework.
(
4
)
adopted ITD for hypergradient computation and proposed the OBBO algorithm along with its stochastic variant, SOBBO. Subsequently,
(
30
)
introduced a momentum-based search direction and proposed the SOGD also in stochastic environment.
(
3
)
studies non-stationary functional bilevel optimization, which can also be analyzed within the existing OBO framework.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notations and Assumptions

Let
\|\cdot\|
denote the
\ell_{2}
-norm of the vector.
For the inner-level function in problem (
1
), we denote the partial derivatives of
g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
with respect to
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{y}
by
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
, respectively.
We also use
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
to denote the Jacobian of
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
with respect to
\mathbf{x}
and the Hessian of
g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
with respect to
\mathbf{y}
.
For the upper-level function in problem (
1
), we denote the total derivative of
f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
by
\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
.

We impose the following Lipschitz continuous and convex assumption for problem (
1
) as follows.

Assumption 2.1
.

For all
t\in[T]
and given
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, we suppose
g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
\mu_{g}
-strongly convex in
\mathbf{y}
.

Assumption 2.2
.

For all
t\in[T]
, we suppose: (i)
f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L_{f,0}
-Lipschitz continuous and
\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L_{f,1}
-Lipschitz continuous; (ii)
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L_{g,1}
-Lipschitz continuous; (iii)
\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
and
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
are
L_{g,2}
-Lipschitz continuous.

\kappa_{g}:=L_{g,1}/\mu_{g}
is used to denote the condition number of
g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
with respect to
\mathbf{y}
. We also impose the standard upper bound assumption on the outer objective function value, as commonly used in online non-convex optimization.

Assumption 2.3
.

For all
t\in[T]
, there exists a positive constant
Q
such that
\left|f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))\right|\leq Q
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
.

2.2
Online Bilevel Optimization

We consider the same
bilevel local regret
as in
(
30
)
, which is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\!\!\!\mathrm{Reg}(T)=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\!\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}\!.\!

(2)

Here the notation
\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}
presents the gradient mapping with respect to
\mathcal{X}
, that is

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{g},\gamma\right):=\frac{1}{\gamma}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}^{+}),

for given
\mathbf{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}
and
\gamma>0
, where

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{+}=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\{\left\langle\mathbf{g},\mathbf{u}\right\rangle+\frac{1}{2\gamma}\|\mathbf{u}-\mathbf{x}\|^{2}\right\}.

(3)

The analysis of this regret relies primarily on the following two variation regularities for describing function changes, which are also used in
25
;
4
;
30
.

\displaystyle V_{T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\!\left|F_{t-1}(\mathbf{x})-F_{t}(\mathbf{x})\right|\quad\text{and}

(4)

\displaystyle H_{p,T}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x})-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\right\|^{p}.

(5)

2.3
Hypergradient-Based Algorithm

This work primarily uses AID to approximate the hypergradient, which is more efficient than ITD proven by
21
. According to properties of implicit functions
10
, the hypergradient admits the closed-form expression

\displaystyle\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}),

(6)

where
\!\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})))^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))\!

can be viewed as the solution to the quadratic function

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}({\mathbf{x}}),\mathbf{v}),\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}):=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),

(7)

and
\mathcal{V}=\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\,|\,\|\mathbf{v}\|\leq L_{f,0}/\mu_{g}\}
under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
.
By solving the subproblem in (
1
) and (
7
) to obtain the termination points
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{*}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{t}^{*}
, respectively, the following computable formula:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{t}^{*})=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{*})-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{*})\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{t}^{*}

(8)

can be used to approximate
\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))
. Below we present a general definition of online hypergradient-based algorithm class.

Definition 2.4

(Online Hypergradient-Based Algorithm Class)
.

Suppose there are totally
T
time steps, the iterates
\{(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\}_{t=1}^{T}
are generated according to
(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t},\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}
with
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{x}}^{1}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{1}=\{0\}
.

For all
t
, the linear subspace
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}
is allowed to expand
K
times with
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t,1}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t}
for
k=1,\dots,K
, shown as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t,k+1}\leftarrow\mathrm{Span}\big\{

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{i},\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{i}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i})\big\},

where
\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{x}}^{i}
,
\mathbf{y}_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t,j}
and
i\in[1,t]
,
j\in[1,k]
.

Then with
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t+1}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{t,K}
,
S>0
,
\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t}
expand as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{x}}^{t+1}\leftarrow\mathrm{Span}\Big\{\mathbf{x}_{i},\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}f_{i}(\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j}),\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{i}(\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{j})\prod_{j=1}^{s}\left(\mathbf{I}_{d_{2}}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{i}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{s},\mathbf{y}_{j}^{s})\right)\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{i}(\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{i})\Big\},

(9)

where
\mathbf{x}_{i},\widetilde{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\overline{\mathbf{x}}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{s},\widehat{\mathbf{x}}_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{x}}^{i}
,
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j},\overline{\mathbf{y}}_{j},\mathbf{y}_{j}^{s},\widehat{\mathbf{y}}_{j}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathbf{y}}^{j}
with any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
for
i\in[1,t]
,
j\in[1,t+1]
and
s\in[1,S]
.

Our definition can be seen as an online extension of Definition
2
in
19
, they shown that the form of equation (
9
) can simultaneously summarize the hypergradient computation of both AID and ITD methods.

3
Online Non-Convex Bilevel Optimization under Standard Bilevel Local Regret

In this section, we study the online hypergradient-based algorithms under standard bilevel local regret.

3.1
Adaptive Inner-loop Iteration Method Achieves the Optimal Regret Bound

In OBBO
4
, each round
t
requires fixed
O(\log T)
times of inner-level gradient queries with fixed step size schedule to keep the error within a suitable range. However, this static inner iteration strategy struggles to handle adversarial inner function changes. When the difference between
g_{t}
and
g_{t-1}
is small, additional iterations will cause the optimizer to oscillate around
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})
; when the difference is large, the error will also be large.

3.1.1
Adaptive inner-loop Algorithm design

Inspired by the follow-the-leader iteration form used in
(
15
;
17
)
, we propose our Adaptive inner-loop Online Bilevel Optimizer (AOBO), which uses a dynamic inner iteration strategy to track the drift of
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
in each round
t
. Consider in time step
t
, we use simple gradient descent method to solve the subproblem, starting with
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{1}=\mathbf{y}_{t}
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k}).

(10)

Due to the
\mu_{g}
-strongly convexity of
g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\cdot)
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|
can be reduced by a constant ratio
\rho\in(0,1)
with a fixed step size
\alpha
. We use the computable current inner-level gradient norm to measure this difference,

\displaystyle\mu_{g}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k+1})\right\|.

The error tolerance parameter
\delta
can be set to determine when a good approximate solution
\mathbf{y}_{t+1}
has been obtained, thus the final
\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|
can be controlled by

\displaystyle\mu_{g}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|\leq\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|\leq\delta.

(11)

Under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
,
we can verify the solution
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
to the linear system (
7
) always lies in the Euclidean ball
\mathcal{V}=\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}\mid\|\mathbf{v}\|^{2}\leq L_{f,0}/\mu_{g}\}
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
. Projected gradient descent can be applied to optimize
\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\cdot)
begin with some
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{1}=\mathbf{v}_{t}
and repeating
M
times, shown as

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m+1}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{V}}\left(\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m})\right).

(12)

Finally, with
\widetilde{\mathbf{y}}_{t}^{*}=\mathbf{y}_{t+1}
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{v}}_{t}^{*}=\mathbf{v}_{t+1}=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{M+1}
, we use (
8
) to calculate
\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})
and update
\mathbf{x}_{t}
by (
3
), shonw in Algorithm
1
.

Algorithm 1

Online Adaptive inner-loop Bilevel Optimizer (AOBO)

Input
: Initializations:
\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
.

Parameters
: step size
\alpha
,
\gamma>0
, iteration number
M
.

Output
: Decision sequences:
\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
,
\{\mathbf{y}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
.

for

t=1
to
T

do

2:

Output
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
, receive
f_{t}
and
g_{t}

3:

Set
\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{t}

4:

while

\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|>\delta

do

5:

\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\alpha\nabla g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})

Set
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{1}\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{t}

for

m=1
to
M

do

10:

end

for

11:

Calculate
\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})
by (

) with
\mathbf{v}_{t+1}=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{M+1}
and use it to update
\mathbf{x}_{t+1}

3.1.2
Bilevel Local Regret Analysis

The regret guarantee for OBO algorithm hinges on how to control the accumulated hypergradient approximation error. The following lemma provides a key upper bound.

Lemma 3.1
.

Under Assumption
2.1
,
2.2
, let
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
and
\beta\leq\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\Delta_{G}\leq

\displaystyle c_{\mathbf{y}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}+c_{\mathbf{v}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|,

where
c_{\mathbf{y}}
and
c_{\mathbf{v}}
are some constants and

\displaystyle\Delta_{G}:=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))-\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}.

The difference
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|
can also be bounded by the following lemma.
The difference
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|
can also be bounded by
\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|
and a term controlled by
M
.
Thus with (
11
), by choosing an appropriate error tolerance
\delta
,
\Delta_{G}
can be compressed to a constant order via our dynamic inner-loop iteration strategy. The following theorem provides more details.

Theorem 3.2
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.3
, let
\alpha\leq\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
,
M=\left\lceil-\frac{\ln T}{\ln\rho}\right\rceil
and
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{2L_{F}}
, Algorithm
1
can guarantee

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}(T)\leq O\left(1+\delta^{2}T+V_{T}\right),

where
L_{F}
is the smoothness parameter of
f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
w.r.t.
\mathbf{x}
. The total number of inner iterations
\mathcal{I}_{T}
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{T}\leq O\left(T\log\delta^{-1}+H_{2,T}\right).

Note that setting
\delta=1/\sqrt{T}
, we can guarantee that Algorithm
1
satisfies
\mathrm{Reg}(T)\leq O(1+V_{T})
with
V_{T}=o(T)
, which matches the lower bound
\Omega(1+V_{T})
proved by
(
14
)
in single-level online non-convex optimization. Therefore, we naturally have the following theorem.

Theorem 3.3
.

Consider any algorithm
\mathcal{A}
that satisfies Definition
2.4
with any
K,S>0
, under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.3
with
d_{1}=d_{2}\geq\Omega(1+V_{T})
, there exist
\{f_{t},g_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
that satisfy
V_{T}=o(T)
for which
\mathrm{Reg}(T)\geq\Omega(1+V_{T})
.

Theorem
3.3
shows that our AOBO achieves the optimal bound. However,
H_{2,T}
does not admit a linear upper bound
O(T)
implied by smoothness assumptions, i.e.,
V_{T}\leq 2QT
under Assumption
2.3
. The drift of
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
over
t
can be arbitrarily large, which means that there is no finite upper bound on
\mathcal{I}_{T}
, but
\mathcal{I}_{T}\leq O(T\log T)
holds as long as
H_{2,T}\leq O(T\log T)
is assumed.

Remark 3.4
.

Intuitively, dynamic inner-loop iterations remove the dependence of the regret upper bound on
H_{2,T}
, at the cost of additional gradient queries and computational overhead. We additionally provide in Appendices
B.5
and
B.6
the results of SOBOW and OBBO under the standard bilevel local regret. The results show that, compared with AOBO, their upper bounds still depend on
H_{2,T}
.

3.2
Fully Single-Loop Method with Regret Analysis

Although we obtain the optimal regret bound by AOBO, it may be difficult to deploy in practice due to its substantial computational overhead.
Existing algorithms either rely on a large
O(T\log T)
number of inner-level gradient queries or incur extremely high
O(T^{2})
computational complexity (Table
1
).
Is it possible to obtain an sublinear guarantee on fully single-loop method with
O(T)
computational cost?

Based on above, we propose a simple Fully Single-loop Online Bilevel Optimizer (FSOBO) as shown in Algorithm
2
, similar to Algorithm
1
, it uses (
10
) and (
12
) to solve the subproblem and (
7
) with both once time. Our analysis shows that, in deterministic OBO, the fully single-loop structure admits a simpler and clearer upper bound.

Lemma 3.5
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, consider the inner iteration process of
\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\cdot)
in Algorithm
2
. Here for simplicity, we define
\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}:=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})
and if set
\beta\leq\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
we can obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\rho_{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}+c_{1}\left(\|\mathbf{x}_{t-1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}c_{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2}

(13)

\displaystyle+c_{2}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2},

(14)

where
\rho_{\mathbf{v}}^{\prime}\in(0,1)
,
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are some constants.

Lemma
B.6
shows that although solving the linear system (
7
) only once can incur a large error, introducing the additional gradient-variation regularities in (
13
) and (
14
) allows this error to be partially captured, which is crucial for our proof. Therefore, the key point is to control the variation of the upper-level decision difference
\left\|\mathbf{x}_{t-1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\|
and the inner-level
\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\right\|
, which can be achieved by imposing appropriate restrictions on
\gamma
and
\alpha
. The following theorem gives our final result.

Algorithm 2

Fully Single-loop Online Bilevel Optimizer (FSOBO)

Input
: Initializations:
\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
.

Parameters
: step size
\{\alpha\}_{t=1}^{T}
,
\beta>0
,
\gamma>0
.

Output
: Decision sequences:
\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
,
\{\mathbf{y}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
.

1:

for

t=1
to
T

do

2:

Output
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
, receive
f_{t}
and
g_{t}

3:

\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{t}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})

4:

\mathbf{v}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{V}}\left(\mathbf{v}_{t}-\beta\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t})\right)

5:

calculate
\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})
by (
8
) and use it to update
\mathbf{x}_{t+1}
by (
3
)

6:

end

for

Theorem 3.6
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.3
, let
\beta\leq\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
,
\alpha\leq\frac{2\kappa_{F}\mu_{g}^{2}}{(\mu_{g}+L_{g,1})(\kappa_{F}\mu_{g}^{2}+C_{\beta})}
,
\gamma<\min\left\{\frac{1}{2L_{F}},\sqrt{\frac{(1-\rho)C_{1}}{12C_{\mathbf{x}}}}\right\}
, Algorithm
2
can guarantee

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}(T)\leq O(1+V_{T}+H_{2,T}+E_{2,T}),

where
C_{1}
,
C_{\beta}
,
C_{\mathbf{x}}
are some constants,
E_{2,T}:=E_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y},T}^{g}+E_{\mathbf{y},T}^{f}
and

\displaystyle E_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y},T}^{g}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2},\quad E_{\mathbf{y},T}^{f}:=\sum_{t=2}^{T}\sup_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{2}.

(
30
)
proposed another fully single-loop method named SOGD and derived
O(T^{1/3}(\sigma^{2}+V_{T})+T^{2/3}\Psi_{T})
under stochastic OBO with noise variance
\sigma^{2}
. This bound relies on stronger sublinear assumptions, in particular, if
\Psi_{T}=O(\sqrt{T})
, term
T^{2/3}\Psi_{T}
becomes
O(T^{7/6})
and breaks the bound. We attribute this to the combined effects of large hypergradient errors and stochastic gradient noise. Our results complement a more robust result for the fully single-loop algorithm in a deterministic environment. However, it is possible that SOGD has a tighter upper bound of a similar form without any stochastic noise, and we provide more details in Appendix
B.7
.

4
Online Non-Convex Bilevel Optimization with Window-Averaged Bilevel Local Regret

Existing regret analyses rely on specific regularity measures of environmental variation, and assumptions typically require these variations to grow sublinearly. These assumptions are inherently unobservable in advance and largely beyond our control. In this section, we introduce a new analysis framework to remove this fragile dependence.

4.1
Regret Definition and Preliminaries

We first introduce a new
window-averaged bilevel local regret
in the following definition.

Definition 4.1

(Window-Averaged Bilevel Local Regret)
.

Given a window size
w\leq T
and weight parameter
\eta\in(0,1)
, consider the averaged objective functions:

\displaystyle\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=0}^{w-1}\eta^{i}f_{t-i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\quad\text{and}\quad\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\frac{1}{W}\sum_{i=0}^{w-1}\eta^{i}g_{t-i}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}),

(15)

where
W:=\sum_{i=0}^{w-1}\eta^{i}
and
f_{t}\equiv 0
for
t<0
. The optimum on the averaged inner-level function in (
15
) is represented by
\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=\arg\min_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
. Our window-averaged bilevel local regret is defined as

\displaystyle\text{Reg}_{w}(T):=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}.

Our regret is intuitively appealing: for linearly changing functions, it is impossible to obtain sublinear guarantee on standard local regret, we resort to evaluating the algorithm’s performance over a time horizon rather than by an instantaneous criterion at the current round
t
. The environment still changes sublinearly within a suitable
w=o(T)
.

Our definition is similar to the definition (
14
) in
(
35
)
, however, they do not take into account the averaging of the inner functions, which partly explains the additional dependency of their results on
H_{1,T}
and
H_{2,T}
, which describe the drift of
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
with time step
t
.

Now we consider the window-averaged hypergradient.

\displaystyle\nabla\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}\widehat{g}_{t,w}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))+\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}}^{2}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))=0,

which follows from
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))=0
and reveals the closed form of window-averaged implicit gradient
\nabla\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
.

\nabla\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
is then obtained by

\displaystyle\nabla\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))=\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))-\nabla_{\mathbf{x}\mathbf{y}}^{2}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))\mathbf{v}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x})

and
\mathbf{v}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
is equivalent to the following solution:

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}):=\mathop{\arg\min}_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\widehat{\Phi}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{v}),\quad\text{where}

(16)

\displaystyle\widehat{\Phi}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}):=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{f}_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}).

Since the unlimited adversarial drift of
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
can prevent the algorithm from approximating it in finite time, i.e., for the same
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})
can be infinitely far apart, we make the following assumption for our method:

Assumption 4.2
.

For all
t\in[T]
and given
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
, there exists a finite constraint domain
\mathcal{Y}
that satisfies
\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{Y}
. And exists
D>0
if
\mathbf{y}
,
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y}
, then
\|\mathbf{y}-\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\|\leq D
.

4.2
Window-averaged Online Bilevel Optimizer

Algorithm 3

Window-averaged Online Bilevel Optimizer (WOBO)

Input
: Initializations:
\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
.

Parameters
: step size
\alpha>0
,
\beta>0
,
\gamma>0
.

Output
: Decision sequences:
\{\mathbf{x}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
,
\{\mathbf{y}_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
.

1:

for

t=1
to
T

do

2:

Output
\mathbf{x}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{t}
, receive
f_{t}
and
g_{t}

3:

Set
(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})\leftarrow(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},\mathbf{v}_{t})

4:

while
condition (
18
) is not met
do

5:

\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})

6:

\mathbf{v}_{t+1}\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{\Phi}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})

7:

Calculate
\widetilde{\nabla}\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})
by (
17
) and use it to update
\mathbf{x}_{t+1}
by (
3
)

8:

end

while

9:

end

for

According to our regret definition, the algorithm needs to find a stationary point of the exponentially weighted objective
\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
over a time window.
Similarly, we use gradient descent to solve the averaged inner-level subproblem and linear system (
16
), shown as:

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k+1}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{k})

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m+1}

\displaystyle\leftarrow\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{\Phi}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}).

Note that the constraint set
\mathcal{Y}
in Assumption
4.2
is prior information, so the algorithm is not allowed to directly apply projected gradient methods over it.
The hypergradient used to update
\mathbf{x}
can be calculated by the following formula.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\nabla}\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{x}}\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mathbf{v}

(17)

We extend the dynamic inner-loop iteration idea in Algorithm
1
and aim to capture the sublinear environmental variation within a given time window. However, a single-loop hypergradient update alone cannot guarantee this easily. Inspired by
(
14
)
, we construct the following error tolerance condition

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\nabla}\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}+\kappa_{F}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}\widehat{g}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}+8\kappa_{g}^{2}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\widehat{\Phi}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{W^{2}}

(18)

to build our final Window-averaged Online Bilevel Optimizer (WOBO) shown in Algorithm
3
. The essence of this condition is to use a computable coarse upper bound of
\nabla\widehat{f}_{t,w}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t,w}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
at each step
t
to capture a sequence
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v})
that makes the hypergradient sufficiently small. The following theorem establishes an upper bound on our window-averaged bilevel local regret for Algorithm
3
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.3
and
4.2
, let
\beta=\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
,
\alpha=\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
and
\gamma\leq\min\left\{\frac{1}{4L_{F}},\frac{1}{456\kappa_{g}^{4}L_{g,1}L_{\mathbf{v}}\sqrt{\kappa_{F}}}\right\}
, Algorithm
3
can obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{Reg}_{w}(T)\leq O\left(\frac{T}{W^{2}}\right)\!\quad\text{and}\!\quad\mathcal{I}_{T}\leq\frac{8QW^{2}T}{\gamma\delta^{2}}+O(T),

where
L_{F}
,
L_{\mathbf{v}}
and
\kappa_{F}
are some constants,
\mathcal{I}_{T}
represents the total number of inner iterations.

Sublinear bound can be guaranteed if
W=o(T)
. The following theorem shows that our method achieves the optimal bound on our regret.

Theorem 4.4
.

Consider any online hypergradient-based algorithm, under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.3
and
4.2
with
d_{1}=d_{2}\geq\Omega(T)
, given window size
w>0
and
\eta\in(0,1)
, there exist
\{f_{t},g_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
for which
\mathrm{Reg}_{w}(T)\geq\Omega(T/W^{2})
.

Proof Outline of Theorem
4.4
.

The key to the proof is to construct a set of functions
\{F_{t}\}_{t=1}^{T}
that change linearly with time. Let
F_{t}(\mathbf{x})=\widetilde{F}([\mathbf{x}]_{t})
,
[\mathbf{x}]_{i}
denotes the
i
-th coordinate of
\mathbf{x}
. Without any prior knowledge, the algorithm inevitably faces an optimization problem at each time step
t
, located at a new coordinate. Therefore the regret
\|\nabla\widetilde{F}(0)\|
at each round is unavoidable, but within a given time window
w
, this regret can be averaged over
W
, leading to our lower bound.

5
Experiments

In this section, we provide the empirical studies on the applications of online data hyper-cleaning and parametric loss tuning for imbalanced data tasks.

(b) corrupted loss against iteration

(c) test accuracy against iteration

Performance of different algorithms on the online hyper-cleaning.

w

(b) regret under different
\eta

Performance of WOBO with different window sizes
w
with
\eta=0.9
and weight parameters
\eta
with
w=5
in subfigures (a) and (b), respectively. The running time of different algorithms in Figure
1
is shown in subfigure (c).

5.1
Online Data Hyper-cleaning

Hyper-cleaning uses a bilevel optimization framework to assign weights to training samples with label noise, down-weighting noisy data and up-weighting clean data to improve model performance. In the online hyper-cleaning setting, where data arrives continuously, the optimization algorithm must be efficient, adaptive, and robust to changing data distributions, ensuring effective noise suppression and reliable real-time learning.

At each time step
t
, the optimizer receives: (i) a small dataset
\mathcal{D}_{t}=\{\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}},\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}\}
, consisting of a noisy training set
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}
with a certain level of label corruption and a clean validation set
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}
; (ii) the current weight parameters
w_{t}(x,y)
, where
x
represents data feature and
y
represents label; (iii) the current model parameters
\theta_{t}
. The model parameters are updated through an inner optimization problem, while the weight parameters are updated via the outer objective, forming a bilevel optimization framework.

\displaystyle\min_{w\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}F_{t}(w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}\right|}\sum_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}}\mathcal{L}(f(\theta_{t}^{*}(w);x_{i}),y_{i})

\displaystyle\mathrm{s.t.}\quad\theta_{t}^{*}(w)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}g_{t}(w,\theta),\quad\text{where}

(19)

\displaystyle g_{t}(w,\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}\right|}\sum_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{L}\left(\sigma(w)f(\theta;x_{i}),y_{i}\right).

Here
\mathcal{L}
denotes the cross-entropy loss,
\sigma(\cdot)
denotes the sigmoid function and
f(\theta;x_{i})
denotes the output of the model extracted from the data feature
x_{i}
. We conduct experiments on the MNIST dataset
23
for an image classification task, using a linear model as the classifier.

We conduct five repeated experiments with a data stream starting from a training set with 10% label noise. Corrupted labels are consistently replaced with a fixed class label (set to 1), causing a stronger misleading effect on the optimizer. Each method runs for 3000 steps, divided into three phases: steps 0–999 with 10% noisy data, steps 1,000–1,999 with 20%, and steps 2,000–2,999 with 30%. We tune all learning rates
\alpha
,
\beta
and
\gamma
in
\{0.001,0.005,0.01,0.05,0.1,0.5\}
for all
t\in[T]
. In Figure
1
, we compare the performance of different algorithms, where AOBO achieve the lowest bilevel local regret accumulation. In Figure
2
, we compare the effects of different window parameters on WOBO. The result shows that larger
w
and
\eta
can reduce the regret, which confirms the conclusion of Theorem
4.3
. Subfigure (c) in Figure
2
shows that our FSOBO achieves the fastest runtime.

(a) regret against iteration

(b) training loss against iteration

(c) test accuracy against iteration

Figure 3:
Performance of different algorithms on the parametric loss tuning for imbalanced data.

5.2
Parametric Loss Tuning for Imbalanced Data

Tuning for imbalanced data focuses on addressing the challenges posed by skewed class distributions in machine learning tasks. In such cases, models, especially deep neural networks with strong fitting capabilities, tend to be biased toward the majority class, leading to poor performance on the minority class.

Parametric training loss can be used to address this issue by balancing accuracy and fairness while preventing overfitting, which is defined as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\gamma,\Delta}(f(\theta;x),y)=-\log\frac{\exp(\gamma_{y}[f(\theta;x)]_{y}+\Delta_{y})}{\sum_{j=1}^{J}\exp(\gamma_{j}[f(\theta;x)]_{j}+\Delta_{j})},

We use this loss to optimize the model’s performance on the training set
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}
and set
\lambda=(\gamma_{j},\Delta_{j})_{j=1}^{J}
, which means that the logits adjustment is treated as an outer variable and optimized by validation set
\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}
. Similar to (
19
) shown as:

\displaystyle\min_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}F_{t}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}\right|}\sum_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}}u_{y_{i}}\mathcal{L}(f(\theta_{t}^{*}(\lambda);x_{i}),y_{i}),

where
\theta_{t}^{*}(\lambda)=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}g_{t}(\lambda,\theta)
and

\displaystyle g_{t}(\lambda,\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}\right|}\sum_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}}\mathcal{L}_{\gamma,\Delta}(f(\theta;x_{i}),y_{i}).

Here
u_{j}
denotes the reciprocal of the proportion of samples from the
j
-th class to the total number of samples
24
. We use a lightweight CNN on MNIST, consisting of four convolutional blocks (a
3\times 3
convolution, ReLU,
2\times 2
max pooling, and BatchNorm), followed by flattening and a final linear layer that outputs
10
logits.

We evaluated performance over
400
timesteps in four
100
-timestep phases, transitioning from
0.6^{i}
to
0.9^{i}
distribution for each class
i=0,1,\dots,9
. Our experimental results are shown in Figure
3
. AOBO achieves the smallest cumulative local regret and the best balanced accuracy on the test set. Moreover, FSOBO attains a smaller cumulative regret than SOGD, which corroborates our theoretical results.

6
Conclusion

We propose a novel algorithm with a dynamic inner-iteration and obtain an optimal bound under standard bilevel local regret. We also provide a tighter upper bound for a fully single-loop algorithm. Furthermore, we introduce a new window-averaged local regret to evaluate performance under linearly-varying environments, derive a lower bound for this regret, and our proposed algorithm is optimal.

Impact Statement

This paper presents work whose goal is to advance the field of Machine
Learning. There are many potential societal consequences of our work, none
which we feel must be specifically highlighted here.

Appendix A
Experimental Details

A.1
Online Data Hyper-cleaning

In this experiment, we utilize the MNIST dataset to simulate an online data hyper-cleaning task with time-varying label noise. The dataset contains 60,000 training images, which are split into a corrupted training set of 45,000 samples and a clean validation set of 15,000 samples. The model employed is a linear classifier with parameters
\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times C}
, where
d=28\times 28=784
and
C=10
.

We introduce systematic label noise to the training stream by flipping the labels of a portion of samples to class ‘1‘. The noise rate
\rho
serves as the environmental variable and changes over time:
\rho=0.1
for
t\in[1,1000]
,
\rho=0.2
for
t\in[1001,2000]
, and
\rho=0.3
for
t\in[2001,3000]
. The online process runs for
T=3000
steps with a batch size of 1000.

The inner objective is to train the classifier on the potentially noisy training data using a weighted cross-entropy loss, where the sample weights are learned to down-weight identified noisy samples. The inner function is defined as:

\displaystyle g_{t}(\mathbf{w},\theta)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}|}\sum_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}}\ell_{\text{ce}}(\sigma(w_{i})f(\theta;x_{i}),y_{i}),

where
w_{i}
is the learnable weight parameter associated with the training sample index
i
, and
\sigma(\cdot)
is the sigmoid function.
The outer objective is to minimize the standard cross-entropy loss on the clean validation set with respect to the weight vector
\mathbf{w}
:

\displaystyle F_{t}(\mathbf{w})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}|}\sum_{(x_{j},y_{j})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}}\ell_{\text{ce}}(f(\theta^{*}(\mathbf{w});x_{j}),y_{j}).

Regarding hyperparameters, we set the inner learning rate
\alpha=0.05
, the linear system learning rate
\beta=0.01
and the outer learning rate
\gamma=0.05
. For AOBO, the inner-loop error tolerance is
\delta=0.1
. For OBBO, we fix the number of inner optimization steps at
K=5
. For SOGD, we set the momentum coefficient to
0.9
. The linear system iteration number are set to
5
for all methods.

A.2
Parametric Loss Tuning for Imbalanced Data

In this experiment, we apply OBO to the parametric loss tuning task on imbalanced data streams. We use the MNIST dataset and simulate a time-varying class imbalance environment. The data stream consists of 400 steps with a batch size of 64, divided into 4 phases of 100 steps each. In each phase, the class distribution of the training data follows a geometric progression
n_{c}\propto\mu^{c}
for class
c\in\{0,\dots,9\}
, where the imbalance factor
\mu
changes sequentially in
\{0.6,0.7,0.8,0.9\}
. The validation set remains balanced throughout the process.

The model is a Convolutional Neural Network (CNN) with four convolutional blocks (
3\times 3
Conv–ReLU–
2\times 2
MaxPool–BatchNorm), followed by a linear classifier. The feature dimension before the linear layer is 64. The inner objective is to train the classifier by minimizing the logit-adjusted cross-entropy loss on the imbalanced training data, which is defined as:

\displaystyle g_{t}(\mathbf{y},\theta)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}|}\sum_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{tr}}}\ell_{\text{adj}}(f(\theta;x_{i}),y_{i};\mathbf{d},\mathbf{l}),

where
f(\theta;x)
denotes the logits output by the model, and
\ell_{\text{adj}}
is the adjusted loss with learnable parameters
\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{d},\mathbf{l})
:

\displaystyle\ell_{\text{adj}}(\mathbf{z},y;\mathbf{d},\mathbf{l})=-\log\frac{\exp(\sigma(d_{y})z_{y}+l_{y})}{\sum_{j=1}^{C}\exp(\sigma(d_{j})z_{j}+l_{j})}.

Here,
\mathbf{d}
controls the temperature-like scaling and
\mathbf{l}
acts as a bias correction. The outer objective
F_{t}(\mathbf{y})
is to minimize the standard cross-entropy loss on the balanced validation set.

\displaystyle F_{t}(\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}|}\sum_{(x_{i},y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{t}^{\mathrm{val}}}\left(-\left[f(\theta^{*}(\mathbf{y});x_{i})\right]_{y_{i}}+\log\sum_{j=1}^{C}e^{\left[f(\theta^{*}(\mathbf{y});x_{i})\right]_{j}}\right).

For all online bilevel optimizers, we set the inner learning rate
\alpha=0.1
and the outer learning rate
\gamma=0.001
. We provide specific hyperparameter settings for the baselines as follows: for OBBO, we set the number of inner optimization steps and Neumann approximation terms to
K=5
; for SOBOW, we solve the linear system using Conjugate Gradient (CG) with
M=5
iterations; for AOBO, the inner loop adopts an adaptive stopping criterion with error threshold
\delta_{t}=0.1
, and the linear system is solved via 5 CG iterations; for FSOBO and SOGD, we update the auxiliary variable for the linear system solution with a learning rate of
\beta=0.001
.

Appendix B
Proofs of Section
3
under Standard Bilevel Local Regret

In this section, we primarily provide proofs of regret upper bound about AOBO and FSOBO characterizing in Algorithms
1
and
2
, and the lower bound of standard bilevel local regret. These proofs do not rely on any window averaging strategy but require certain assumptions about sublinear variations. In Section
B.1
, we list all the lemmas used in this section. Section
B.2
provides a proof of the upper bound of AOBO, Lemma
3.1
is an intermediate conclusion in its proof, in (
44
). Section
B.3
provides the lower bound proof. Section
B.4
provides OFSBO. In Section
B.5
and
B.6
, we additionally prove the upper bound of SOBOW
[
25
]
and OBBO
[
4
]
under standard bilevel local regret. Finally in Section
B.7
, we prove that SOGD
[
30
]
can possibly obtain an improved upper bound in deterministic OBO setting.

B.1
Preliminary Lemmas

Under Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
, the inner function
g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})
is
L_{g,1}
-smooth and
\mu_{g}
-strongly convex w.r.t.
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, we present the additional properties used below.

For all
t\in[T]
, any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbf{y}_{1}
,
\mathbf{y}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}_{d_{2}}
, it holds that

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{2})\right\|\leq L_{g,1}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{1}-\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\|

(20)

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{2})\right\|\geq\mu_{g}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{1}-\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\|,

(21)

also we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{2}),\mathbf{y}_{1}-\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\rangle\geq\frac{\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{1}-\mathbf{y}_{2}\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{2})\right\|^{2}.

(22)

Lemma B.1

(Lemma 16 in
[
35
]
)
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, for all
t\in[T]
, any
\mathbf{x}_{1}
,
\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1}))-\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2}))\|\leq L_{F}\|\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}\|,

where

\displaystyle L_{F}:=L_{f,1}+\frac{L_{f,0}L_{g,2}}{\mu_{g}}+\frac{2L_{g,1}L_{f,1}}{\mu_{g}}+\frac{L_{g,1}^{2}L_{f,1}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{2L_{f,0}L_{g,1}L_{g,2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{L_{f,0}L_{g,1}^{2}L_{g,2}}{\mu_{g}^{3}}.

Lemma B.2

(Lemma 16 in
[
35
]
)
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, for all
t\in[T]
, any
\mathbf{x}_{1}
,
\mathbf{x}_{2}\in\mathcal{X}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1})-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{2})\right\|\leq\frac{L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}}\|\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2}\|.

Lemma B.3
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, for all
t\in[T]
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\|\leq\frac{L_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}},

\displaystyle\quad\text{where }\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}):=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}):=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mathbf{v}-\mathbf{v}^{\top}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}).

Proof.

notice that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))=\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=0,

thus we can see

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=

\displaystyle\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\|=

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|^{-1}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})\right\|\leq\frac{L_{f,0}}{\mu_{g}},

where the last inequality comes from Assumption
2.2
.
∎

The following lemma provides a fundamental bound for solving linear system
\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y},\cdot)
with respect to
\mathbf{v}
.

Lemma B.4
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, consider the inner iteration process of
\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\cdot)
in Algorithms
1
and
2
for all
t\in[T]
, shown as

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m+1}\leftarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{V}}\left(\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\beta\nabla_{\mathbf{v}}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m})\right).

If set
\beta\leq\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
and the above iteration performs
M_{t}
times for time step
t
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{32L_{f,0}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}(1-\beta\mu_{g})^{M_{t}}+\left(\frac{4L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

We can split the bound between
\mathbf{v}_{t+1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))
into two parts - the bound between
\mathbf{v}_{t+1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})
, and the bound between
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})
and
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))
. For simplicity, we define
\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}:=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})
and
\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}:=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))
. It begins with

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\|^{2}\leq 2\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\|^{2}+2\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\|^{2}.

(23)

Let
\mathbf{w}_{t}
represents

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{t}^{m+1}=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\beta\widetilde{\nabla}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}),\quad\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m+1}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{V}}\left(\mathbf{w}_{t}^{m+1}\right).

We begin with

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{m+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{m+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}+\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{m+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}+2\left\langle\mathbf{w}_{t}^{m+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\beta^{2}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m})\right\|^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}-2\beta\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}),\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\rangle.

(24)

Now because of the
\mu_{g}
-strongly convexity and
L_{g,1}
-smoothness of
\Phi_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}),\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\rangle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m})\right\|^{2}

(25)

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}),\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\rangle\geq

\displaystyle\mu_{g}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

(26)

and substitude (
25
), (
26
) into (
24
) to have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\beta^{2}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m})\right\|^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}-\beta^{2}L_{g,1}\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}\left\|\nabla\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle-\left(2\beta-\beta^{2}L_{g,1}\right)\mu_{g}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\beta\mu_{g}\right)\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2},

(27)

and thus we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle(1-\beta\mu_{g})^{M_{t}}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-\beta\mu_{g})^{M_{t}}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}+\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(1-\beta\mu_{g})^{M_{t}}\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}+2(1-\beta\mu_{g})^{M_{t}}\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}.

(28)

Now remind of Lemma
B.3
, we actually have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq 2\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}^{m}\right\|^{2}+2\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}},

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq 2\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}+2\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}},

and substitude into (
28
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16L_{f,0}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}(1-\beta\mu_{g})^{M_{t}}.

(29)

Now we consider about the bound of
\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\right\|
, which begins with

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))-\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right)^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\|^{2}+2L_{f,0}^{2}\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}-\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right)^{-1}\right\|^{2},

(30)

where the last inequality comes from the smoothness of
f_{t}
and
\nabla f_{t}
being Lipschitz continuous, and for the second term, we can obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}-\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right)^{-1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right)^{-1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right)^{-1}\right\|^{2}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}\left\|\left(\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right)^{-1}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}\|^{2},

(31)

where the last inequality comes from Assumption
2.2
,
\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}g_{t}
is
L_{g,2}
-Lipschitz continuous, thus after subtituting (
31
) into (
30
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\left(\frac{2L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{2L_{f,0}^{2}L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}.

(32)

Finally, we substitude (
29
) and (
32
) into (
23
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq\frac{32L_{f,0}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}(1-\beta\mu_{g})^{M_{t}}+\left(\frac{4L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2},

which finish the proof.
∎

The following Lemma
B.5
and
B.6
are used in proving the upper bound of Algorithm
2
. Lemma
B.5
, through the conclusion of equation (
22
), additionally scales out a negative inner-level gradient accumulation term, which is used to partially offset the additional error caused by solving linear system
\Phi_{t}
in a single iteration in Lemma
B.6
.

Lemma B.5
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, consider the inner iteration process of
g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\cdot)
in Algorithm
2
with
\forall\alpha>0
and
N=1
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})\right\|^{2}-\left(\frac{2\alpha}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}-\alpha^{2}\right)\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}{\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\kappa_{g}^{2}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}{\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{x}_{t}-\mathbf{x}_{t-1}\right\|^{2}.

Proof.

It begins with

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\alpha\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\alpha^{2}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right\|^{2}-2\alpha\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}),\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\rangle,

(33)

due to (
22
), let
\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{x}_{t}

\mathbf{y}_{1}=\mathbf{y}_{t}
and
\mathbf{y}_{2}=\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})
, we have

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}),\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\rangle\geq\frac{\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}+\frac{1}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right\|^{2},

(34)

and substitute (
34
) into (
33
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}-\left(\frac{2\alpha}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}-\alpha^{2}\right)\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)(1+\lambda)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})\right\|^{2}-\left(\frac{2\alpha}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}-\alpha^{2}\right)\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\kappa_{g}^{2}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left(1+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left\|\mathbf{x}_{t}-\mathbf{x}_{t-1}\right\|^{2},

let
\lambda=\frac{\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t+1}-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})\right\|^{2}-\left(\frac{2\alpha}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}-\alpha^{2}\right)\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}{\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})-\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\kappa_{g}^{2}\left(1-\frac{2\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}\right)\left(1+\frac{\mu_{g}+L_{g,1}}{\alpha\mu_{g}L_{g,1}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{x}_{t}-\mathbf{x}_{t-1}\right\|^{2}.

∎

Lemma B.6

(Restatement of Lemma
3.5
)
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, consider the inner iteration process of
\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\cdot)
in Algorithm
2
with
M_{t}\equiv M=1
, if set
\beta\leq\frac{1}{L_{g,1}}
we can obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\beta\mu_{g}}{2}\right)\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}+\frac{4(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{2}{\beta\mu_{g}}\right)}{\mu_{g}^{2}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{2}{\beta\mu_{g}}\right)}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{4L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\right)(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{2}{\beta\mu_{g}}\right)\left(\|\mathbf{x}_{t-1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\|^{2}\right),

here for simplity, we define

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}:=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}),\quad\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}:=\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))=\mathop{\rm argmin}_{\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{V}}\Phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}).

Proof.

Similar to the proof in Lemma
B.4
, we divide
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\right\|
into
\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|
and
\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}
and handle them separately.

It begins with (
28
) in Lemma
B.4
but with
M_{t}\equiv 1
in single-loop situation, which shown as

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\beta\mu_{g}\right)\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-\beta\mu_{g})(1+\lambda)\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}+(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\beta\mu_{g}}{2}\right)\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}+(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{2}{\beta\mu_{g}}\right)\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2},

(35)

where
\lambda=\frac{\beta\mu_{g}}{2}
. Now we process
\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}
in two parts separately, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}=

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\underbrace{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})-\mathbf{v}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}}_{(h.1)}+2\underbrace{\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t-1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\mathbf{v}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}}_{(h.2)}.

(36)

For
(h.1)
, we have

\displaystyle(h.1)=

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})-\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left\|\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})-\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left\|\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t-1},\mathbf{y}_{t})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{2L_{f,0}^{2}L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\right)\left(\left\|\mathbf{x}_{t-1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\right\|^{2}\right),

(37)

where the last inequality comes from the Assumption
2.2
. For
(h.2)
, we have

\displaystyle(h.2)=

\displaystyle\left\|\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\left\|\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\left\|\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\left[\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right]^{-1}\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2L_{f,0}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}+\frac{2}{\mu_{g}^{2}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}.

(38)

Substitute (
37
) and (
38
) into (
36
), we finally have

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{4L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\right)\left(\|\mathbf{x}_{t-1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\|^{2}\right)+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{4}{\mu_{g}^{2}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2},

(39)

and substitute (
39
) into (
35
) to obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t+1}-\mathbf{w}_{t}^{*}\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\beta\mu_{g}}{2}\right)\left\|\mathbf{v}_{t}-\mathbf{w}_{t-1}^{*}\right\|^{2}+\frac{4(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{2}{\beta\mu_{g}}\right)}{\mu_{g}^{2}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{2}{\beta\mu_{g}}\right)}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\left\|\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t-1}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})-\nabla_{\mathbf{y}\mathbf{y}}^{2}g_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{4L_{f,1}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{2}}+\frac{4L_{f,0}^{2}L_{g,2}^{2}}{\mu_{g}^{4}}\right)(1-\beta\mu_{g})\left(1+\frac{2}{\beta\mu_{g}}\right)\left(\|\mathbf{x}_{t-1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\|^{2}+\|\mathbf{y}_{t}-\mathbf{y}_{t+1}\|^{2}\right).

(40)

Here we finish the proof.
∎

Lemma B.7
.

Under Assumption
2.3
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right)-f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1})\right)\right)\leq 2Q+V_{T}

where
V_{T}
is defined in (
4
).

Proof.

it naturally holds that

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right)-f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))-f_{t+1}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1}))\right)+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f_{t+1}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t+1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1}))-f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle f_{1}(\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{y}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{1}))-f_{T+1}(\mathbf{x}_{T+1},\mathbf{y}_{T+1}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{T+1}))+V_{T}\leq 2Q+V_{T},

where the last inequality comes from Assumption
2.3
.
∎

The following lemma is fundamental for the proofs of algorithms
1
,
2
and
4
. It reveals that bilevel local regret defined in (
2
) can be upper bounded to the accumulation of function value differences on the sequence
\{\mathbf{x}\}_{t=1}^{T+1}
and the accumulation of hypergradient approximation error. The former has been dealt with by lemma
B.7
above, while the key to the different algorithms lies in the handling of hypergradient error.

Lemma B.8
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, for all
t\in[T]
, we can obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\gamma\theta}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))-f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1}))\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\gamma\theta}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left\|\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))-\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})\right\|^{2},

where
\theta=1-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{\gamma L_{F}}{2}
for some constant
\lambda>0
.

Proof.

We begin with the smoothness of
f_{t}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}))
, given by Lemma
B.1

\displaystyle f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1})\right)-f_{t}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\langle\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\mathbf{x}_{t+1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\rangle+\frac{L_{F}}{2}\left\|\mathbf{x}_{t+1}-\mathbf{x}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\gamma\left\langle\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\rangle+\frac{\gamma^{2}L_{F}}{2}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\gamma\left\langle\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\gamma\left\langle\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})-\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\rangle

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma^{2}L_{F}}{2}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2},

then with Young’s inequality,

\displaystyle f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1}))-f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\gamma\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma\lambda}{2}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma}{2\lambda}\left\|\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})-\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma^{2}L_{F}}{2}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\gamma\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{\gamma L_{F}}{2}\right)\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2\lambda}\left\|\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})-\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2},

(41)

for
\forall\lambda>0
. We also have

\displaystyle-\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma)-\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma)\right\|^{2},

(42)

and substituting (
42
) into (
41
), it follows that

\displaystyle f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1}))-f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma}{2}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{\gamma L_{F}}{2}\right)\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\gamma\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{\gamma L_{F}}{2}\right)\left\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma)-\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}\left(\mathbf{x}_{t},\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1}),\gamma\right)\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{\gamma}{2\lambda}\left\|\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})-\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle-\frac{\gamma}{2}\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{\gamma L_{F}}{2}\right)\|\mathcal{G}_{\mathcal{X}}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t})),\gamma)\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\gamma\left(1-\frac{\lambda}{2}+\frac{1}{2\lambda}-\frac{\gamma L_{F}}{2}\right)\left\|\widetilde{\nabla}f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t+1},\mathbf{v}_{t+1})-\nabla f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right\|^{2}.

Let
\theta=1-\frac{\lambda}{2}-\frac{\gamma L_{F}}{2}
and summing from
1,\dots,K
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t+1}))-f_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},\mathbf{y}_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{t}))\right)

\displaystyle\leq
